786. THOMAS GLOVER was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously cutting and wounding Samuel Robinson upon his left cheek, on the 16th of Feb., with intent to diafigure him;—2 ND COUNT, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
SAMUEL ROBINSON . I am a labourer, and live in Baker's Rents, Rotherithe, I know the prisoner—he is a tallow-chandler—he lived in the same house with me—had absented himself from home six months—he came back last Monday evening week (I and he had no quarrel before he went away—I cannot assign any reason for his going) he only came to call on us to kick up a row, and then go again—I was in the house in the lower room—his wife was in the same room with me and his son, a little boy—when I saw him come in he was intoxicated, and I said, "Now Tom what have you come about"—I spoke first—Isaid, I hoped he had not come to quarrel, he said, "No," and put out his hand and shook hands with me, and then turning to his wife said, "You b—y b—h," or "w—e, I have come to see you"—It was then proposed to send for a pot of beer, and he gave his son half-a-crown to go and fetch the pot of beer during the absence of his son for the beer he got up and began to strike his wife, and I said, "Now Tom you shan't do that"—when he went to strike his wife he went nearer to her, and I said he should not—he then put his hand in his bosom and pulled out a something—I did not know what it was then—he
flung it at me with all his force, and said, "There you b—y rogue take that"—a deal table, about three quarters of a yard wide, stood between us—it took effect where you see, on my cheek—it did not stick in, but fell down—I did not know at the time what it was—the policeman when he came in picked it up, and found it was a sailor's knife, open—he did not do anything else to me—I then, finding myself hurt, picked up an iron saucepan, and menaced him out of the room—I told him to go, and gave him his hat and he went clear away from the house—I did not give him in charge—he was taken the same night—the policeman picked up the knife about the place where it had fallen, after it had taken effect on my face—I got some warm water and bathed it, it bled very much, but I did not go to a doctor for twenty-four hours after that—he told me to put a plaster on it, which I have done ever since.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You have known him for some years? A. Yes, he and his wife—had been very kind to me on occasion, when I was in some difficulties for debt—his wife had gone out for a policeman before this occurred—he had threatened her—he appeared to come in in a friendly way, but he was in a passion, for he almost broke the door in—I thought he was friendly with me—I cannot say what induced him to make use of those vulgar expressions—it was on turning round and seeing his wife—he had got some fancy in his head about his wife and me—he has had that fancy for five years before—he always struck her when he came of late—I thought he attempted to strike her at this time, which induced me to interfere to prevent him—it was then he threw this knife that struck me—I have known him long—I believe he intended to do me some serious hurt at the time, in consequence of his repeated threats—he often threatened me before I interfered with him and his wife, but not at that time—the wife had been living under the same roof with me when he had been absent—I always have lived there for nine years past—we have been living in the same house together for nine years—when I interfered to prevent him, I put my hand up, and said, "Don't strike her;" and then he drew this knife from his bosom, and said, "Take that"—he on one occasion did find me kissing his wife—the fact is, that after their kindness I became an inmate of the family, and by his consent I was introduced to his acquaintances as the brother of his wife, and he introduced me among his friends and relations as his brother-in-law—we lived under the same roof on those terms about nine years—about five years ago, when living in Pimlico, I came home in the evening, having taken a little more than I usually did, I was rather fresh, and she had sent me up something very comfortable while I was at work, and I said, "Mother, I must have a kiss;" and he was in the yard, and happened to come in at the time—nowthat is all the circumstance he grounded his suspicion on—that is five years ago, and since that we have been on intimate terms—we have lived together as comfortably as we possibly could, and now he turns round and says he had jealousy on this score five years ago—there was no other foundation for it.
GEORGE BATSFORD ( police-constable M 70.) I came to this house on the 16th of this month, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening—I heard a woman cry, "Murder!" and ran there—I cannot say whether the door of the house was open—I met the prisoner's wife before I came to the house—she came out, crying "Murder!" and applied to me to come—I met the prisoner before I got to the house, and asked what was the matter; and he said, "Here I am, if you want me"—at that time I did not know that anything had been done—I took him back to the house—he did not go inside—hestood outside along with another constable, whom I asked to mind him while I went in—when I got into the house I saw the prosecutor—his face
was bleeding—I only found him in the room—there was no child there, nor any one but himself—this was on the ground-floor—he had a cut under the left ear—I searched on the floor, and found this knife open—it is sharpish at the end, more sharp at the end than it is anywhere—it was not quite so clean as it is now—it looked as if it had been wet and dropped down on the dirt—there is a bit of gravel stuff on it—I took the knife, and went out, and asked the prisoner if it belonged to him—he said, "Yes"—I then asked the prosecutor if he was going to charge him—he said, no, he was not; and I told the prisoner to go—he said, "I will not go, I will murder the b—y lot before I go; I have been longing to wash my hands in his blood, and I will murder the lot before I go"—I then took him in charge myself—he was worse for liquor—next morning, as I was taking him to the police-court, he made an observation that he would murder the lot before he went—he was solid and sober then.
Cross-examined. Q. Is there a string attached to the knife? A. Yes—part of the string was attached to the knife, and a part to his button hole—it is a knife he was in the habit of wearing.
MR. MURDOCK. I am a surgeon. The prosecutor applied to me on Wednesday evening, the 18th, two days after the wound had been inflicted—I found an incision on the left cheek, cleanly cut, about an inch long, and penetrating through the skin and the fat beneath the skin—I should say the depth of the wound was about a seventh or eighth part of an inch.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 32.— Confined Three Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18460223-787]
787. WILLIAM WINTON and CHARLES COOPER were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the counting-house of the Governors and Guardians of the Poor of the parish of St. Mary, Newington, and stealing therein 6 half-crowns and 1 sixpence, their monies.
RICHARD SINGLETON . I am foreman of the stone-yard of the parish of St. Mary, Newington. The prisoners have worked there for some months—I receive money from the officers of the parish every morning to pay the men employed in the stone-yard—on the 14th I received 17 s. 6 d. for that purpose, six half-crowns and a sixpence—I put it inside this book, and put it in a counting-house in an inner shed, which is connected with the stone-yard—the prisoners were at work there that day—the dinner time is from one to two o'clock—the men went to dinner at that time; and I changed one half-crown to give the men some halfpence, and put the remainder in the book—I locked both the door of the counting-house and the outer door shed—I have the charge of the counting-house for the parish—it belongs to the stone-yard, which belongs to the governors and guardians of the poor—I left the keys at the lodge, leaving five half-crowns and a sixpence in the closet—I returned at two o'clock, found the bolt pushed back of the outer door shed, and also the bolt of the counting-house door—I went to the closet, and found the money gone—the prisoners had gone away, and did not come back to finish their work—they had a small portion of work to do, which would have entitled them to 1 s. each—they had worked in the yard the day before, and remained to the stone-yard to the dinner hour that day—on missing the money, I went for a policeman, went with him, and found the prisoners at their lodging, in the Mint—I gave Winton in charge—I then went to a building, and called "Cooper"—I got no answer—I went up, and found him there—he must have heard me call—they were searched at the station-house, and 10 s. 5 1/2 d. found on the two—I was present—neither of them had the same dress on as they worked in.
Winton Q. On the Saturday morning, what money did you draw from the officer? A. 17 s. 6 d.—I took out a half-crown to change, and put the remainder of the change in the book—I kept the halfpence in my pocket, about 3 d. or 4 d.—I paid 1 s. 2 d. away—a knife was found on Cooper I believe—you never left of an afternoon without being paid for your day's work—thegates had not been left unlocked during the dinner-hour for weeks previous to this transaction, and the men are never there unless I am present with them—there never was any money left in the shed in the morning—there has been at dinner-time—I never left strange men at work in the yard.
JAMES BAKER ( policeman.) On Saturday, the 14th, I went with Singleton to the Mint, and found both the prisoners there—Winton first—Cooper, I believe, ran out of the house—Singleton brought him to me—I took both to the station—in going along Winton said to Cooper, "They cannot swear to the money"—I searched Cooper at the station and found on him a knife, two half-crowns, sixpence, a penny, and one halfpenny; and on Winton, one half-crown, two shillings, and fourpence—I asked why they left the workhouse—theysaid they had heard of another job—I asked where—they said at the docks—I asked which docks—they could not tell me—I went to the work-house, examined the outer shed, and saw some sharp instrument had hooked the bolt of the lock back, and the marks corresponded with the knife—I examined the counting-house door, and found a piece of wood had been cut away by screwing the knife round, and there is a little gap in the knife—I tried both the locks to see if I could put them back with the knife after they were locked, and I did.
MARY DAGO . I am a widow, and live in the Borough—I sell secondhand clothes—on Saturday, the 14th of Feb., the prisoners came to my shop together—Cooper bought this coat of me, and Winton bought a jacket.
WILLIAM CHANDLER . I live in Ball-alley, Mint—I was at work at the stone-yard—I left at one o'clock to go to dinner—I returned to work at two o'clock, and as I went I met the prisoners coming away from the workhouse—theytold me they had got a job and were not coming to work this afternoon—I asked where the work was—they did not tell me, and said I might take some of their wood to make up my lot.
Winton's Defence. We left work at one o'clock, and in going along, we went into the Ship and Blue-coat-boy, and just as we got to the Elephant and Castle met a gentleman with two boxes; we asked if we should carry them; he said, "What will you take them to the Dover Railway fort?" I said 1 s.; he said, "Make haste;" when we got there he gave us 18 d.; we thought it not worth while to return to work, being so late; we are quite innocent of the crime.
Cooper's Defence. I left the yard with Winton, intending to return; we called at the Ship and had a pint of beer; we came out and said we did not think we should return that afternoon; we went to the Elephant and Castle and met a gentleman with two boxes; we carried them to the Dover railway, and he gave us 18 d.; I then said to Winton there was a man over the water that owed me 2 s. and I should go and get it; we were sitting down to tea when Singleton and the officer came in; I did not run up stairs; I walked up; whether Singleton and the officer called once or twice I do not know; I heard him the last time; I came down with him, and was accused of stealing the money; I said "It is false;" we went down there; on the first examination Singleton said he had lost 14 s. 6 d.—I am entirely innocent; Singleton drinks very much, and occasionally leaves the door open.
WINTON — GUILTY . Aged 23.
COOPER — GUILTY . Aged 26.
Recommended to mercy.— Confined Eighteen Months.
Before Mr. Justice Mattle.
[trialAccount t18460223-788]
788. FREDERICK MUNTON was indicted for feloniously assaulting Johanna Munton, and cutting and wounding her in and upon her neck, with intent to murder her.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD VASEY . I am a cabinet-maker and joiner, and live at No. 13, Crosier-street, Lambeth. The prisoner is married to my sister—they live it No. 3, Halford-terrace, Walworth—I lodged in their house from Jan. to Feb.—on Sunday, the 25th of Jan., in the morning, as I was lying in bed I heard them quarrelling below—it was about half-past seven o'clock, or day-break—I judge that was the time—we had no clock or watch in the house—my mother came into my bed-room—I heard them quarrelling and a severe struggling between Mr. and Mrs. Munton—when the struggling was all over, I heard a voice at the foot of the stairs—I sent my mother down, and told her to call me if I was wanted—my mother went down, and about two minutes afterwards she called me—I went down and saw the prisoner stark naked and covered with blood—I asked him what was the matter—he made no answer—Isaw my sister, and I asked her who did it—she was lying on the ground with her throat cut in the centre, and she feebly pointed to him—I went to the street door and found it double locked and the key taken away—I on that asked the prisoner for the key—"I do not know" was his reply—"You have concealed the key" I said to him—"I do not know" was his reply again—I asked him to lend me a hand to take down the board in front of the parlour window that he was in the habit of cutting out his cloth upon—the white roller blind was down and marks of blood on it—I pulled it off the roller altogether and threw it into the corner of the room—we took the wire blind down, he raised the sash and I got out of the window and called the policeman—Dr. Otway was sent for—he came and attended her for the wound—there was blood on the panels of the folding doors as if squirted upon, and marks of fingers, and likewise round the room, on the looking-glass and on some tea and sugar that laid on the mantel-piece—they have latterly lived on quarrelling terms, anything but happiness—I have heard him use threatening language to her—I heard him go out on the Thursday nigbt previous and return home about one o'clock on Friday morning—when he came home I heard a quarrelling and a knock as if somebody was knocked down, and I went and demanded admittance at the parlour door—it was locked—I heard him knock her down—I went to the street door to call "Police," when my sister came out and put her hand before my mouth—I asked her if he had struck her—I asked her that twice, and she answered "No no, never mind, never mind"—I then went up stairs into my own bed-room, and then I came down and listened to them, and he wanted her to take a false
oath against me—she said, "I cannot, I will not take a false oath against my brother," and he replied, "Then I will do for you"—there had been some understanding between me and the prisoner previous to this—my mother was examined before the Magistrate—she has died since—she died on the 6th of Feb.—I can repeat several other former things.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. The misunderstanding between you and the prisoner resulted in his summoning you before the Magistrate? A. No, the misunderstanding was caused through my taking my sister's part—I never was before the Magistrate—I had been summoned before this matter took place—the summons was to have been heard two or three days afterwards, on the Tuesday afterwards—this occurred on the Sunday—I am quite sure I did hear what I have stated—he wanted her to take a false oath against me, to swear I had committed an assault on him, which I had not done—the summons was for an assault—she said she could not take a false oath against her brother, and then he said if she did not he would do for her—I heard that distinctly—there were other wrangling words—afterwards I heard him say, "I will take a warrant out against you, I will transport you for seven years, if you do not take a false oath against your brother"—hesaid that several times, for I listened for two hours—he was intimidating her—they were quarrelling for upwards of two hours that night—he said he would transport her if she did not take a false oath against me—he said that first when I knocked at the door, "If you don't take a false oath against your brother I will do for you"—I stated that before the Magistrates—I lived in the house with him—he was the landlord—my mother lived there too—I have had frequent quarrels with him, but not with my sister, merely as you would say tiffs—when she had come up into my room I have ordered her out—I have ordered her out of the room, and just put my arm to put her out—I have not shoved her down stairs—Ihave not been threatened by the prisoner that he would have me up before a Magistrate for assaulting his wife—never for anything of the kind, not for assaulting his wife, only for assaulting him—he never attempted to assist her—he never complained of my assaulting her, or pushing her out of the room—my mother was not, as I am aware of, fond of interfering in their domestic arrangements—there were not constant complaints of her conduct made to me—I have never heard the prisoner say that he had no peace through his wife's mother—I have not heard him complain of her interference, nor of my sister's—my mother had not complained to me about it, nor has asked me to interfere—the prisoner and my sister occupied the back parlour, bed-room, and kitchen, and middle room on the first floor—I remained there after this matter took place up till the 16th of Feb.—I was there when the surgeon came—I did not, to my knowledge, see my sister shake hands with the prisoner—I cannot charge my memory that I ever saw her do so on that morning—I do not know whether it might have occurred, and I forget it—I would rather swear it did not occur in my presence, but I would rather give him the benefit of the doubt—I cannot recollect it—it was nothing very particular to observe—I did not hear him say anything to his wife—I did not hear him say to his wife, "How did this happen?" or, "What did it?"—I only heard him repeat that once—I cannot recollect whether she did or did not hold out her hands and shake hands with him—when he said "Who did it?" she was lying on the floor, and she took no notice—that was the only time I heard him—I did not hear him say, "Who did it?"—I was not present then—Jonathan Cook was there—I fetched him—I was in and out of the parlour because I fetched Dr. Otway some water and some sponge—I could not have been there the whole time—I said at once that I thought he had cut her throat, from the threatening language—I did not say that he had done it—I might have said so, but I cannot recollect whether I did or did not.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Did this assault which has been spoken of at all arise from any misunderstanding of that kind, about taking your sister's part? A. It arose out of this, one Wednesday morning she came up into my room and said, "My life is in danger," I thought it very strange for her to male that observation, and as I did not wish to interfere between man and wife I said, "Go out of my room," I looked over the bannisters and saw him going into the parlour, and I called him a cowardly villain—he took out a summons against me on the 21st, and it was served on me on the 22nd—I was in and out during the time Cook was there—the shaking hands might have taken place without my seeing it.
JONATHAN COOK ( police-constable P 254.) On Sunday evening, the 25th of Jan., I was fetched to the prisoner's house—I tried to get in at the front door—I could not—it was locked—I was obliged to get in at the window—I found the prisoner's wife lying bleeding, with her throat cut, on her back, in the parlour, near the parlour fire-place—the prisoner was holding her hands, apparently to keep her still—he had his shirt on, and a very great deal of blood upon it—she had her night-gown on and stockings, but no night-cap—the prisoner cried out to me what was he to do—Mr. Sharland, the milkman, was there and the brother—I said, "Send for the doctor immediately," and we sent for one—at the time the man was gone for the doctor the prisoner asked his wife who did it—she gave no answer, but looked very hard—I asked her who did it, and she looked very hard but gave no answer—she looked at both of us—we were both before her—I found this razor at the right side of the woman, and the left side of the man, about as far from her as she could reach with her right arm, and about where he was kneeling with his left knee—it was not quite close—there was blood on the handle, as it is now, and partly closed—Mr. Otway came to the door—he tried to come in—I asked the prisoner for the key—he said he did not know where it was he was sure—Mr. Otway got in at the window afterwards—after she was removed to the bed I asked the prisoner where the key was, and he went to his coat pocket and took it out—when I asked him before, he said he did not know where it was, for he was out having a glass or two on the Saturday night—after that the prisoner asked her who did it, and I asked her who did it, and she laid still and looked very hard—she then rose herself up in the bed and shook hands with him, and kissed him, and pointed and showed him where some money was, which he found under the bed—I saw him take it from there—some pen, ink, and paper were given to her a few minutes after—the prisoner was there at that time—she then wrote these words on this paper—(producing it)—I took the prisoner into custody—she held up her fist at me, as though she dared me to touch him, and then I put my hand out and shook hands with him.
Cross-examined. Q. And when you did that she seemed pleased, did she not? A. I did not see any difference—she laid still and looked hard—when I took hold of her husband she still laid still—she first of all shook her fist at me, but after I shook hands she laid still—When she shook her fist at me I was not about to take him—I was talking to him, and she shook her hand at me and then put her hand out as though she would shake hands with him—she did before that, and kissed him—not then—Mr. Otway was in the room at that time, and the witness Vasey—the kissing and shaking of hands attracted a great deal of attention from all of us—we were all looking at it—I am quite sure it took place, and not very likely I should forget it—the prisoner appeared in a very distressed state to me—no doubt he was—he kept saying, "Who did it, who did it? how was it done?"—he said so three times.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. I suppose you were paying particular attention to the state of the woman? A. I paid attention to the woman and the man too—Icannot undertake to say what was seen by other persons—at the time of her shaking her fist at me I had not made any attempt to take the prisoner into custody—I only told him before her, that he must not leave the room—I was in my uniform.
ANN COX . I am the wife of John Cox, a brass-finisher. I am housekeeper to a person of the name of Coxhead, and live at No. 2, Halfordterrace, next door to the prisoner—I recollect on the morning of the 25th of Jan. hearing a noise, which was, I should say, about five minutes to eight o'clock—on my word I cannot describe what noise it was—I never heard
such a racketting in my life—it was a confusion, and I heard him say, "You b----b, you will halloo murder, will you?"—I cannot say it was the prisoner—Iheard a confusion in the passage of the house No. 3, and I heard a man's voice—I think it was the prisoner—they wrestled from the passage to the parlour, and then she must have clasped her hands by the manner of the sound—she said, "Oh you villain, you villain; you said you would; what have you done? you have done it at last"—I then heard her fall—I consider it was Mrs. Manton's voice—I am certain it was a woman's—he had beaten her on the Tuesday morning and on the Thursday night dreadfully—I only heard it from the sound the same as I heard then—he beat her shout half-past ten on Tuesday morning—I heard it again on Thursday night, at least I may more properly say Friday morning.
Cross-examined. Q. You seem to know all about your neighbour? A. No I do not—I do not know what they are doing all day at No. 1—I was first called on to give evidence on the 26th I believe—I did not come forward myself—inspector Collier found me out—I happened to repeat the words what I had heard when I went for our dinner ale, and then they came and called for what I had said—I have not been to a public-house to-day—I made the observation to I believe that gent —I did not think anything serious would have come of it, or else I would not have got into this mess I can assure you—I know he did the deed, although I did not see him do it—I heard quite sufficient—I do not know how the inspector found me—you had better ask him—I was brought here unwillingly—I came to speak the truth, and I do not care for any one—I told the people at the public-house what I thought proper—I did not suppose I should be brought here or I would have told them more—I have told you the whole truth and nothing but the truth—I do not drink gin.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. You went to the public-house for some ale? A. I did—I mentioned there what I had heard, and in consequence of that Collier called upon me.
JOHN SHARLAND . I am a milkman, and live at No. 26, Canterbury-place, Walworth. On the Sunday morning I saw Mrs. Munton's brother running towards Kennington-road, calling for the police—I went to the prisoner's house, and saw him leaning his body out of the window, beekoning for assistance to come—he was perfectly naked at that time—he came to the window and said, "Come in for a witness, as I did not do the deed"—he said to me, "Put the question to her, and ask whether I cut her throat or not"—I made no reply—I put no question whatever, and he said, "My dear, did I cut your throat? did I cut your throat, my dear?" and she shook her head—themother came in, and said, "You villain, you said you would murder her, you said you would;" and the brother said the same, because she would not take a false oath against him—the mother told me that she came down and asked who did it; and that the poor creature rose her hand and said he done it—that was said in the prisoner's presence—he made no answer—I do not doubt but that he heard it, for in the confusion the mother was still beating him with her hand, and said, "You villain, you said you would do it, you said you would do it."
Cross-examined. Q. She was striking him, was she? A. Yes—that was after his wife shook her head and said he had not done it—when I came in the prisoner was quite naked—the mother came in afterwards—the prisoner came from the window where the blind was—he removed the blind aside—the mother seemed in very great agitation—I do not consider the prisoner was so much agitated as the mother and the son—they were scolding him—the brother said very little—I did not see Mr. Otway come in—I thought I would escape out
of the mess altogether, as soon as the policeman had come, and I put my foot out of the window—I did not see Mr. Otway at all—one of the expressions the prisoner used was, "Who did it? before it is too late"—she appeared it that time to be sinking rapidly, and the blood was flowing rapidly—I heard the mother say, "You villain, you said you would murder her"—I did not hear her say, "You have done it"—there was a great deal of confusion in the room at that time—the prisoner was stark naked, and bloody from the crown of his head to the sole of his feet—I got is at the window, the same as I got out—before that I had heard the brother hallooing for a policeman, from where I was—I was on my business at the time—when I got in at the window the blind was down, or partly down, and a great deal of blood about it, the prisoner came and pulled it on one side—it was a roller blind—when I went there the blind had not been pulled off the roller—it was not done while I was there—it was there when I went out.
RICHARD VASKY re-examined. My mother gave her evidence at the police-court, and it was taken down in a book—her name was Susannah Vasey.
LLEWELLYN GUNN . I am clerk to the Magistrates at Lambeth. I recollect Susannah Vasey giving her evidence on the 20th of Jan., in the prisoner's pretence—I took down her statement in this book—she was sworn—( read)—"Susan Vasey, sworn, says, 'I am a widow, and live at No. 3, Halford-terrace, Penton-place, Newington. The prisoner is married to my daughter, Johanna; I have lived with them nearly a month; he has been married three years; he has very much ill-used her; the first thing yesterday morning I heard the prisoner call "Murder!" that was a little after eight o'clock yesterday morning; I ran down stairs, and met the prisoner at the foot of the stairs, without his shirt;. his wife was lying down on the floor, with her throat cut, and bleeding; I said to my daughter, "Who did it?" I and she pointed to the prisoner, and said, "You did it;" I slapped him on the shoulder as he passed, and said, "Oh, Fred, put your shirt on! look at your hands!" my daughter fainted away; he made no observation, but ran into the back parlour; he was sober; he came home about half-past twelve the night before; they slept together in the back parlour; it was in the front parlour she was on the floor; she had only her night-clothes on; I picked up a razor that lay on the left side of my daughter, on the floor; I called, "Police!" and gave the razor to, the policeman; the prisoner ran into the back parlour, and fastened himself in; I think the prisoner heard his wife say he had done it, and he made no observation, but ran out of the room; I called to my son; the key of the front door was lost, and I was obliged to open the window to key people in; I did not hear the prisoner say a single word; they had been quarrelling last Thursday night, and I heard her say, "I will not take a false oath for any. one," and I heard him say he would do for her; her face was dreadfully bruised some time ago; he has been in the habit of ill-using my daughter.
Cross-examined. I and my son lodged there, at 3 s. a week; I am quite sure that the remark, "I'll do for you," I was not made by my son; I have not heard my daughter say anything about who did it since yesterday morning; I remained ten minutes with my son in the front parlour; when I first entered the room my daughter was on the floor; I did not observe her attempt to pull the wound open; her hands were lying down by her sides; her eyes were rolling when I first saw her on the ground, and she appeared quite powerless; she did not make use of any words but "He did it;" she raised up her handy, and said, in a low voice, "He did it;" I do not know if my son has received any notice from the prisoner to quit; I know that the prisoner took out a summons against my son.'"
Cross-examined. Q. Was this taken down word for word as the woman made the statement? A. It is the original statement taken down by me, word for word as it took place—she was not examined on any subsequent occasion.
CHARLES COXHEAD . I live in Great Bath-street, Clerkenwell. On Sunday, the 25th of Jan., I lived at No. 2, Halford-terrace, Penton-place—Mrs. Cox is my servant. About twenty minutes or a quarter past seven o'clock I heard in the adjoining house a man and woman quarrelling and fighting in the passage—I distinctly heard two voices, male and female, but the parties are strangers to me—I know it came from No. 3—I heard a great deal of most foul and abusive language—I heard the man distinctly say, "D—n you, you will halloo murder again, will you," and I heard the woman immediately afterwards give a kind of shriek—they appeared to me then to retire into the front parlour—they appeared to be in the passage before—I went up stairs, and looked out at the first-floor window, and heard somebody call for assistance, for the doctor, and police, and call murder, but I did not go in.
Cross-examined. Q. You keep the Ben Jonson? A. Yes—Mrs. Cox was not with me when I heard this—I was in the front parlour—as soon as murder was called, there were five or six people collected—I was not flurried.
CHARLES WILLIAM OTWAY . I am a surgeon, and live at No. 11, Canterbury-row. On Sunday, the 25th of Jan., I was called to No. 3, Halfordterrace, and saw a woman lying in the front parlour—the prisoner was by her side, with his left hand on her throat, and his right hand in one of her hands—he desired me to come in, and said, "You see what this has come to, Mr. Otway"—(I had been called in two days before on account of a quarrel which had taken place in the family, and I then gave them some advice, and left them)—I had the woman removed on to the bed, and examined her throat—it was a short, clean wound, about an inch and a half long, half separating the windpipe—blood was issuing from it, and also passing down the throat—it was such a wound as would be likely to be caused by such an instrument as this (a razor)—she did not appear quite sensible when I first saw her—I dressed the wound after some time—the prisoner was very much agitated during the whole time when the expressions were made use of by her friends—the wife made signs to me, intended to lead me to the opinion that she did it herself, and she several times shook hands with her husband, and caressed him—after she recovered a little, she spoke to me, but she could only speak in a whisper—I said if she had anything to say, she had better write it down, as she ought not to speak, but she said, "I did it myself"—she repeated it in a low tone—a paper was given to her, and she wrote on it—this is it—she at first wrote—( reads) "I did it myself through distress of mind; pray, my dear Fred., take care of our dear Arthur, for your poor Ann's sake; will you be father and mother for ever; do not charge my mother and brother with anything, they are innocent"—she signed that in the presence of the policeman and myself, at our request—I said to her, "Your mother and brother are not charged with anything; you have heard what your mother hat said, have you anything to say about your husband?"—she in great excitement wished to write again—I had the brother and mother, with the policeman, present, and she wrote these words, "Nor my husband," in their presence.
Q. What is your opinion as to the mode in which the wound was inflicted? A. I consider a razor must have been taken, and the head thrown back so as to protrude the throat, and the razor just pushed there—I think if a man had taken the razor, he would take a sweep as if cutting his own throat, but a female would handle a razor in a very different manner.
COURT. Q. You think it was given by herself? A. I do—I think if it had been done by another person, it would have been rather a larger wound—if it had been deeper, it would have cut through the large blood-vessels—this had just cut the windpipe—it was not such a sweeping wound as would have been done by a man—if there had been any resistance or struggling, I think there would have been more wounds, or it would have been an irregular wound—it would be very likely to be a clean cut—I form my notion that it was done by herself, from the small extent of the wound, and the cleanness of it.
Cross-examined. Q. If there had been considerable struggling previous to and at the time the wound was inflicted, it was not at all likely to have presented these appearances? A. Decidedly not—if it was inflicted by a man intending to cut her throat, it would have been a larger and stronger wound—ifthe head had been down, it would have cut through the carotid artery—the head being thrown up, would throw the windpipe forward, and that was cut through—there was no danger to her, except from the excitement the friends got her into—the mother was very greatly excited, and I told her if she was quiet I should be obliged to have her removed—she was saying he was a villain, and he did it—I had been called in on the previous Thursday, in consequence of a quarrel between the mother and brother and her—the prisoner himself sent for me then—I had never seen him before—he was not a patient of mine—he exhibited on that occasion affection and interest about his wife—she was then very excited, rushing out of bed, throwing herself on her knees to her mother, and begging her to leave the house—she exhibited no want of affection towards her husband—I believe the mother said they had been so often quarrelling, and the brother also, and he said he would not leave the house, being determined to protect her, but the whole time she was begging them to leave—it is decidedly my belief, having examined the wound, that it was inflicted by her own hands.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. How long were you there on the Thursday? A. I stopped a quarter of an hour—I understood that the quarrel was between the prisoner and Mrs. Munton and her family.
ARTHUR JOHN VASEY . I am the son of the prisoner's wife, and live with my aunt, at Shaftesbury-terrace—I recollect the Thursday night before this Sunday—I slept in the room with the prisoner and my mother—he came home at one o'clock at night—I went to bed in the dark in the back room—he and my mother were quarrelling in the front room—I heard a fall in the front room, and I thought it was my mother falling—they afterwards came into the back room where I was, and came to bed—I slept in the same bed with them—there was a rushlight burning—I saw him get out of bed and snuff it with a pair of scissors, and he put the scissors to his own throat, and said be should do for her—I afterwards saw my mother lying on the floor.
Cross-examined. Q. Who did you tell this story to first? A. The Magistrate—my aunt Gillon I told first—I cannot recollect when—she did not ask me about it—I went and told her at her house—she came for me, and took me away—she did not ask me if they had quarrelled together—she asked me whether I saw Mrs. Munton on the floor—I told her yes—I do not know how she came to ask me that—she did not say anything to me about whether Mr. Munton had ever threatened his wife—I have been living with her ever since—I do not recollect when I told her this story the last time—I do not think I told it her yesterday—she told me to speak the truth—I did not tell her all I had to say before I came here this morning—I think I did not—I do not think she told me it was not quite right—she never set me right—I do not think I made a mistake when I told it her last—she is sister-in-law to Mr. Munton—I have heard her say she did not like him—she has not read over
anything to me—she took down what I was to say, and kept the paper—she did not show it to me afterwards—she showed it to the Magistrate and to Mr. Gillon, and he put it into the fire.
MR. ROBINSON Q. Your aunt came to fetch you away on this morning? A. Yes—I told her what I had heard on the Wednesday—she wrote it down, and I went before the Magistrate
ENOCH DAWSON HOWITT . I am a surgeon, and live in Apollo-buildings, Walworth—on Sunday, the 25th of Jan., I was called to the police-station, and saw the prisoner—he stripped before me—I found two scratches, as if by fingernails, just below his right arm, a bruise at the back of the right arm, a bruise and scratch on the same elbow, on the left arm two scratches and a slight one below the elbow—the three first fingers on the left hand were cut, and the cuts were evidently made with the broad part of a sharp instrument—they were cut across about one-eighth of an inch—had I heard nothing of the transaction, I should have said a razor would have done it—his shoulders were very much discoloured and bruised, and he was very much discoloured with blood—he said he was in the habit of sleeping without his shirt, and then he was stopped from saying anything more by the police, or he would have stated more—I saw his wife before I saw him—I think about half-past five in the afternoon—I did not see the wound in her throat—I saw the strapping—Inever saw the wound—I removed two strips of plaster to enable me to form some idea of the extent of the wound—I could see there was not a wound greater than the plaster—it extended very little beyond the windpipe—I could form a notion as to the position of the wound.
COURT. Q. Could you tell that there might be a wound below that, and if so that it could not have been inflicted by the party herself? A. It is impossible for me to give an opinion—I could give an opinion the same as Mr. Otway has, if he is capable of forming an opinion whether the woman or the man committed it—I can form no opinion of it myself.
JOHANNA MUNTON examined by MR. BALLANTINE. I know the charge made against my husband.
Q. On the night of this unfortunate occurrence did you cut your own throat, or did he do it? A. Yes, I did it myself—I have never said from the beginning to the end of the transaction that anybody did it but myself—my husband came out to me while I had the razor in my hand—I do not know whether he forced it out of my hand—I became insensible—I saw him come to me—he came close up to me—whether he forced the razor out of my hand I do not know—my husband never desired me to take a false oath against anybody.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. There was no quarrel between you and him that morning? A. None.
JOHN COLLIER ( policeman.) I went to the house and examined it—I found blood in different parts of the room—I searched the prisoner at the station and saw marks of blood down his shirt and under his shirt, on his body down to his feet—he was covered with blood—there were scratches on his back—I called Mr. Howitt to examine him—the scratches appeared fresh, between his shoulders and on his arm.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

792. FREDERICK JOHN BARNES, JOSEPH HENRY ADOLPHUS BARNES , and CHARLES CARELESS CORK , were indicted for stealing 84 cheroots, value 4 s.; the goods of George Rolls.
GEORGE ROLLS . I am a grocer and live in Red Lion-street, Richmond. On the 15th of Feb., about half-past six o'clock, Frederick John Barnes came into the shop to ask the price of bread—I was in the back parlour—he did not buy anything—a quarter of an hour afterwards the policeman came in, and I missed a box of cheroots—he produced this box, which I believe to be mine—it was safe a quarter of an hour before.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How do these differ from cigars? A. In the shape—I suppose there are thousands of such boxes.
STEPHEN CREED ( police-constable V 200.) On Sunday evening, the 15th of Feb., about a quarter past six o'clock, I was on duty in Red Lion-street, Richmond—I saw the three prisoners in company, come up close to the prosecutor's house, they remained there three or four minutes—Joseph H. A. Barnes and Cork then came eight or ten yards down the street from the shop—and Frederick J. Barnes came away from the door and joined the other two prisoners, who were in sight of the door—they then all took to their heels and run away—I pursued them into King-street, where I took the two Barnes'—Cork made his escape—I found this box of cheroots on J. H. A. Barnes; I asked him where he got it from, he said his brother gave it him—F. J. Barnes said he had been to Mr. Rolls' for a halfpenny worth of figs, and took the box of cheroots and gave them to his brother.
Cross-examined. Q. How far were you from them? A. About the length of this Court—I took Cork the same evening, between nine and ten o'clock.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
F. J. BARNES — GUILTY . Aged 13.
J. H. A. BARNES — GUILTY . Aged 15.
CORK — GUILTY . Aged 16.
Recommended to mercy. Confined Two Days and Whipped.

[trialAccount t18460223-794]
794. FREDERICK STENT was indicted for embezzlement.
JAYNE PAYNE . I am landlady of the Hare and Hounds, at East Sheen, the prisoner was in my employ—I supply Mrs. Hampton with beer, which came to 8 d. every week-day, and 9 d. on Sundays—I supplied her every day from Sept. the 9th, till the 12th of Feb.—I supplied her on the 12th of Feb.—Idid not get the money for that day's beer till the prisoner was accused, and then he took 1 l. 8 s. from his pocket, expressed his sorrow, and said that was part of what he had taken, and he had spent the remainder—the money due to me was 5 l. 6 s. 2 d., and the last that was owing was the 8 d. for the last day, the 12th of Feb.—he ought to have brought me the money every day, from the 9th of Sept.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. There was no regular account was there? A. There was a bill made out at Christmas—he expressed his sorrow and gave up 1 l. 8 s.—he was a very good boy.
JAMES HAMPTON . I have had beer from Mr. Payne—I paid the prisoner regularly every day, except what we had on Sunday, which we paid on the Monday—I paid him 8 d. myself on the 12th of Feb.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460223-895]
895. MARY ANN SMITH was indicted for stealing 32 yards of printed cotton, value 18 s., the goods of George Thomas Minor; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
HENRY EDWARD STREET . I am in the employ of George Thomas Minor, a linen-draper, who lives at Lambeth. On the 30th of Jan. I put four pieces of printed cotton outside the window—I saw them safe between eleven and twelve o'clock—I went to take them in in the afternoon, and missed one of them—this now produced is it—it is in two pieces now.
SOPHIA COLE . I am a pawnbroker. I took in this piece of print of the prisoner on the 30th of Jan., between six and seven o'clock in the evening.
WILLIAM AMOR . I am a pawnbroker. I took in this piece of cotton of the prisoner on Friday, the 30th of Jan.
HENRY EDWARD STREET re-examined. These are my master's property.
JURY. Q. Are you able to swear to it? A. Yes—I believe the prisoner was in the shop with another person, just before I missed the cotton—I swear to this by the pattern, and it has been compared with a piece I cut off it, and it corresponds exactly—I do not suppose only one person had the pattern—this pattern I have in my pocket corresponds with the width of this cotton, and it was torn off a dress that I cut off this cotton—there were thirty-nine yards and a half of it, and I cut off six yards—I should not like to swear that the prisoner was in the shop, but I could swear to the person who was with her.
The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that she had met her sister by Bishopsgate Church, and received the cotton from her to make dresses, and wanting to make a purchase at a bazaar, had pledged it.
THOMAS WILLIAM RADFORD ( police-constable L 52.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at Newington—( read—Convicted 14th April, 1845, and confined six months)—the prisoner is the person,
GUILTY . Aged 43.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18460223-896]
896. WILLIAM LAPHAM was indicted for stealing 1 horse, value 5 l.; the property of John Byars: and 1 cart, 2 l.; and 5 sacks, 5 s.; the goods of John Byars and another, his masters.
JOHN BYARS . I am a baker, and live in Southampton-place, Nine Elms. I have been in the habit of employing the prisoner—on the 22nd or 23rd of Jan. I sent him out on various jobs, and the last time was with the horse and cart and five empty sacks—I gave him 3 s. 6 d. in silver to go for some saw-dust—he never returned—he ought to have returned the same evening—the horse and cart have not been found.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. When did this happen? A. About the 22nd of Jan.—it was on a Thursday—he was apprehended about the 10th of Feb.—he had been with me about two months, backwards and forwards—he eat and drank the same as I did—I had known him about eight months—this horse was Mr. Haywood's, but he lent it to me that I could let the prisoner go out—I am in partnership with my brother James—if the horse had
been lost we should have been liable to pay for it, because we are in joint partnership.
COURT. Q. Was the prisoner acting for your brother as well as for you? A. Yes, the cart was our joint property—the sacks belonged to my miller—they were in my trade, and I was answerable for them—the horse was lent to me, only on condition that my brother should sanction it.
ALFRED SPICE ( police-constable V 41.) I took the prisoner for embezzlement—I did not say anything about the cart and the sacks—on the road to the station he said he sold the horse and cart at Smithfield, but he was tipsy at the time.
ROBERT PARFETT . I sell horses in Smithfield—the prisoner was standing at the hospital gate one afternoon, about three o'clock, with a black poney and a set of harness—I asked him who it belonged to—he said to himself, and he wanted to sell it—he said he would sell it for 6 l.—I said if I could get a customer who would give him 5 l. 10 s., might I have what I could get over—he said I might—he said he had been selling sawdust.
GUILTY of stealing the cart and sacks. Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18460223-897]
897. MORRIS JOHN KNIGHT was indicted for stealing lib. of cigars, value 8 s., and 1/2lb. of cheroots, 4 s., the goods of James Bennett.
ELIZABETH CAROLINE BENNETT . I am the wife of James Bennett, tobacconist, Belvedere-road, Lambeth—on the 11th of Feb., about half-past two o'clock, I was in my kitchen—I heard a bell ring which is attached to the shop-door—I went up and saw a young man in the shop—he asked for a cigar—I served him, and he left—I closed my gate after him and returned to my kitchen—I had not been there three minutes before I heard a noise—I went up and found the prisoner behind the counter—I am sure he is the person—his handkerchief was on the counter with three bundles of cheroots in it, which had been removed from the window—he had got hold of 8 fourth bundle in the window, and some of the cigars had been removed—I attempted to seize him—he knocked me backwards and ran out—I pursued—he was taken in Waterloo-road—I am sure he is the person—I never lost sight of him.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear I stole those that were in the handkerchief? A. They had been removed from the window into the handkerchief.
Prisoner's Defence. I was half a mile from the shop; a man came and said, "You have knocked a woman down;" I said, "No", I have not, I will go back with you;" I went back and saw this woman; she said, "That is him."
Witness. I am positive he is the person—I have not the least doubt of it.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.

a sheet to me, in great distress, and asked me if I would give her 6 d. for it—I gave the same sheet to the officer.

[trialAccount t18460223-802]
802. ANN MILLER was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. CLARKE conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY NUNNERY . I keep the Half Moon, at Rotherhithe. On the 2nd of Feb. the prisoner came to my house for a 1 d. worth of shrub—she tendered me a sixpence—I said, "That is a bad one, and you know it"—she said it was all she had, and begged me to give it her back—I would not—I put it into a little snuff-box on a shelf in the bar—my daughter who serves in the bar saw me put it there—the next time I touched the box was when the policeman came to me on the 12th of Feb.—I marked it and gave it to him—nobody but my daughter and I knew the sixpence was there—I kept no other money there.
AMILIA NUNNERY . I saw my father put the sixpence in the box and put it on the shelf—I did not touch it till the policeman came—I was in the bar when the prisoner came—I am sure she is the person.
JAMES PHILLIPS . I keep the White Horse in Queen-street, Rotherhithe. On the 12th of Feb. the prisoner came in the afternoon, with another person about the same age—the prisoner asked for half-a-quartern of gin and paid a shilling—I looked at it and said, "It is a bad one"—she said she did not know it—I said, "Don't you recollect being at my house on Monday week tod giving my wife a bad shilling"—she said "No"—to the best of my knowledge I had seen her there before—I called the policeman and gave him the shilling—I bad bent it and marked it with my pen-knife—this is it.
JOHN BAILEY WELLS ( police-constable M 54.) I took the prisoner from Mr. Phillip—Nunnery marked this sixpence, and gave it me.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. This shilling and sixpence are both counterfeit.
Prisoner. You will do me a great favour to transport me; I have no friends, and must return to the same bad line as before.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Month.
[trialAccount t18460223-803]
803. JOHN WARD was indicted for a like offence.
MR. CLARKE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN WOODLEY SMITH . I keep the Spread Eagle, at Rotherhithe. On the morning of the 9th of Feb., the prisoner came for a pint of porter, and took out a sixpence to pay for it—I took it up, Writ in two, and gave it him back—he was very abusive, and I sent for the policeman—he had taken up the two pieces of the sixpence, and when the officer came the prisoner put them down on the counter—the officer took them.
CHARLES SCOTT ( police-constable M 245.) I went to Mr. Smith's on the 9th of Feb., and he gave the prisoner in charge for passing a bad sixpence—I found these two pieces of a sixpence on the counter—I took the prisoner to the station, and took 10 1/2 d. in coppers from him—there was a something rattling in his mouth—we tried all means to get it out, and could not—the sergeant took a key, put it into his mouth, tamed it round, and out fell a bad sixpence—I saw another piece in his mouth, which we consider he swallowed, as we saw no more of it.
Prisoner. I sat down on a chair, and opened my mouth, and you broke
two of my teeth. Witness. You opened your mouth after putting the key in—wewere nearly three-quarters of an hour before we could get it.
HENRY HARDING MIDDLETON ( police-sergeant M 2.) I searched the prisoner, and got this sixpence from his mouth, which we opened by great force—heappeared to have another hard substance in his mouth, and to swallow it.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not tell me to sit down in a chair? A. No, I had you put in the chair, and held by two men, while I opened your mouth—youappeared to be drunk, but after we took the sixpence, you were perfectly sober.
MR. JOHN FIELD . These two pieces are parts of a counterfeit sixpence—this other sixpence is counterfeit, and I believe cast in the same mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I had 5 s., 6 d., when I went out; I met with two or three men, and did not go to work; I generally put money into my mouth; I knew nothing about having the coppers; if I had wanted to get rid of the money, I had time enough to have swallowed both of them.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18460223-804]
804. FANNY GOLDSMITH , and MARY ANN GOLDSMITH , were indicted for stealing 251bs. weight of coals, value 4 d.; 13lbs. of candles, 6 s.; 81bs, of soap, 3 s.; 1 yard of carpet, 6 d.; 8 candlesticks, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 butter-stamp, 6 d.; 1 cup, 3 d.; 3 jugs, 1 s.; 2 towels, 8 d.; 1 printed book, 3 d. 1 roasting-jack, 6 d.; 1 brush, 6 d.; 21bs. of coffee, 2 s.; 21bs. of tea, 10 s.; 2lbs. of butter, 2 s.; 14 eggs, 1 s.; 21bs. of sugar, 1 s.; 1 tumbler, 10 d.; 1 pot of jam, 1 s.; 3 basins, 3 d.; and 1 quart of groats, 6 d.; the goods of Angelina Pope.
WILLIAM FULLER ( police-constable P 190.) On Thursday night, the 12th of Feb., I was watching the house of Mrs. Pope, and about eleven o'clock I saw Fanny Goldsmith come from her house, towards Camberwell—I stopped her when she got about 100 yards, and asked what she had got under her cloak—she said, "Some coals and wood"—I said, "Then you must go back with me to the house"—she produced two bags containing coals—she said she had them unknown to any one, and if I would let her go, she would take them back—I took her back, and Mrs. Pope gave her in charge—as I was taking her to the station she dropped a bag—I said, "You have dropped something"—she said, "I do not know anything about it"—it was a bag containing candles and soap—there was another policeman there, but there was no one else who could have dropped it—I took her to the station, and returned to Mrs. Pope's—the prisoner Mary Ann was there, and Mrs. Pope gave her in charge—I was taking her to the station—she said she knew nothing about the things that were taken—she went a little further, and then said, "I suppose we shall both be transported"—I then went with Mr. Campbell to Fauny Goldsmith's house—I found there several articles of stolen property, and some duplicates—these are them.
Fanny Goldsmith. Q. When the policeman followed you from the Rye he said, "Stop, I have something here," and it appeared to be the bag? A. Yes, but I saw it drop from you, and heard it drop—I was turning back to pick it up when the other officer took it.
SAMPSON DARKIN CAMPBELL . I am inspector of the P division of police. I was at the station on Thursday night, the 12th of Feb.—the prisoner Fanny was brought in—I asked where she resided, she gave her address at No. 11, George-street, Camberwell, in the back parlour—she gave up some keys, and said the large key was the key of the parlour door; I should find nothing there but what was her own—I went and found a great quantity of things,
here is a candlestick, some remnants of carpet, a printed book, a roasting-jack, a china-pot, a pot of jam, a jug, and a butter print—the officer who was with me found some duplicates—I found some sugar, some candles, and some coffee, I went back to the station and told Fanny the next morning what I bad found—Isaid, "You said I should find nothing but what was your own"—she said, "I have done very wrong; I was wrong to do it, but distress for want of mans to pay my rent induced me; my child is innocent."
ANGELINA POPE . I am a widow and live at Peckham Rye—Mary Ann was in my service—she came on the 20th of Oct.—Fanny is her mother—on the 12th of Feb. I had a party, and Fanny was there, assisting her daughter in the kitchen—I had only employed her on that, and on one former occasion, but I have known her to be there on Sunday evenings, to see her daughter—these are my articles—I do not know when I lost them—I know I had some of them since Mary Ann has been with me—I kept the grocery goods and candles in a kitchen cupboard—it is usually locked and my daughter keeps the key and gives out the things—on the 12th of Feb. the cupboard was open most of the day—Fanny was at the house that day—she left at night and the policeman brought her back.
The prisoner F. Goldsmith put in a written defence, stating that the sugar found at her house was her own, and some of the articles had been given to her daughter by the prosecutrix.
F. GOLDSMITH — GUILTY . Aged 52.— Confined Twelve Months.
M. A. GOLDSMITH — NOT GUILTY .
(There were two other indictments against F. Goldsmith.)
Before Mr. Recorder.

806. FREDERICK JAMES PEPALL was indicted for stealing 1 grate, value 5 s.; the goods of David Strachan, and fixed to a building.—2 ND COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.—3 RD COUNT, stating it to be the property of William Obadiah Wheeler.
THOMAS WHITCOMB ( police-constable L 89.) On the 3rd Feb. about seven o'clock in the evening I was on duty at Kennington, and saw George Baynes it the corner of Park-street—I heard a whistle and gave him into custody, I then got through an empty house, No. 59, Park-street—I fell over a grate in the yard—I got over to the next house and found the prisoner coming out of the back door with this grate on his shoulder, which I produce—I asked what business he had there—he said, "None"—I asked what he was going to the with the grate, he threw it down and struck me on the wrist with a piece of iron—another officer came up—I secured the prisoner and took the grate to the station—I am sure he had it on his shoulder, and his hands were all over soot found fresh lime, such as a person would meet with in pulling away a grate—I found five other grates had been moved, ready to be taken away, and one tad been taken from Nos. 59, 60, and 61, Park-street.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. All these were empty? A. Yes, they were three empty houses.
DAVID STRACHAN . I am a saddler—to the best of my knowledge, I know the grate produced—it belonged to a house in my possession—I have examined to place it was taken from—it had been fixed and was detached—I claim to hold this house as executor to a person deceased—it was in my legal possession—the houses Nos. 59, 60, and 61, Park-street, are part of the property
of the deceased person—there is a dispute as to the claims of the children which has left this property more immediately under my control till that is adjusted—the prisoner had no right to go to that place.
Cross-examined. Q. When had you last seen those houses? A. Four or five months ago, or perhaps longer—the tenants ran away, with the exception of the prisoner's father, who lived in one of them—I have the keys of some of the houses—a person named Hall has the key of the house from which the grate was taken—he was the tenant—he had paid rent—he owes twelve months' rent now.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Recommended to mercy. Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18460223-807]
807. JOSEPH GIBSON was indicted for bigamy.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM DAVISON DAY ( police-constable K 74.) I apprehended the prisoner at Rotherhithe, on the 12th of Feb.—I told him he was charged with bigamy—he said it would now be settled, and that it was his wish that it should be so—he asked me on our road to the Court what his imprisonment would be—I said I could not tell—he said his second wife knew of his first wife being living at the time, and that she was married again—I went to the church of the Holy Trinity, at Kingston-upon-Hull, on the 19th of Feb.—the minister of the parish showed me the register, and I examined this copy with the orginal—it is correct—this is it—( read —certifying that Joseph Gibson and Mary Walton were married on the 18th of Dec, 1824.)
MARY ANN JORDESON . I am the wife of a person named Jordeson, living at Hull. I know the prisoner—I was present at his marriage with Mary Walton in 1824—she was alive last week, when I left Hull—she has never been from home—I do not think the prisoner and her lived together above three or four years.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. The prisoner is a seafaring man? A. Yes—they lived together three or four years, and had a child—he was away at sea a year and ten months—when he returned his wife was far gone in the family way—that was the reason they parted—they divided the furniture and separated—I believe he has been in Hull, but I did not see him—I do not know that he has been there for seventeen years—his wife married again about two years ago.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was there any difficulty in his finding where his first wife was? A. No.
ELIZA MARY MILES . I am a widow. I married the prisoner on the 1st of July, eight years ago, at St. Saviour's, Southwark—I was in no business—I was protected by my brothers, who are since dead.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner had been living in the house where you were residing? A. Yes, he lodged there—since our marriage my mother and my son have lived in the house—the prisoner has not supported us all—my son has always been employed—I have some property of my own—I married the prisoner in 1838, and have lived with him ever since—my son was married in the summer—I gave him a bed and bedstead on his marriage.
COURT. Q. Did that lead to a family dispute? A. Yes—I understood the prisoner to have been married, and to have been away from home for years, under the circumstances that have been detailed—I expected him to deal fairly by me.
MR. WILDE. Q. Did he not tell you he had had no correspondence with his wife for many years, and did not know whether she was alive or dead? A. Yes—I spoke to Mr. Phillips—he did not advise me to inquire about it—I
did not inquire—I have taken all the furniture from the house, but it was under advice.
GUILTY . Aged 44.— Recommended to mercy Confined Three Months.
