808. WILLIAM BROWN was indicted for stealing 90lbs. weight of lead, the goods of James Courthorpe Peache, and fixed to a building; and that he had been before convicted of felony.—2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.
THOMAS FILES . I am clerk to Mr. James Courthorpe Peache—he is a timber merchant, and lives in Belvidere-road, Lambeth. On the forenoon of Saturday, the 31st of Jan., my attention was called to the outside of the counting-house in the yard—I noticed that some of the lead pipe from the counting-house to the drain was cut away—on the next Monday morning I discovered that some further part of the lead had been removed from the ridge and round the chimney of the counting-house—the officer brought some lead, which turned out to have been removed from there—it was compared, and fitted the place—I had seen it right a few days before—the prisoner has been about the neighbourhood for some years.
JOHN OULD ( police-constable L 87.) On Saturday morning, the 31st of Jan., I was on duty in Waterloo-road—I saw the prisoner and another boy coming along St. Andrew's-terrace—the prisoner had a bag—I spoke to his companion, and the prisoner dropped the bag, and ran away—he was stopped for me, and I took him—I said I wanted him concerning some lead—(I saw that it was lead in the bag)—he aaid, "I know nothing at all about it"—I took the lead to the prosecutor's premises, and compared it with the marks on his counting-house—it fitted exactly.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How far from Mr. Peache's premises did you find the prisoner? A. 500 or 605 yards perhaps—I was gone from the bag for about five minutes to fetch the prisoner—I found it in the same place—I am sure it was the same that he dropped—I left a labourer with, it, who was passing by—he is not here—I looked in the bag as I passed by, and I found it in the same position.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-sergeant L 8.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, at Newington—( read—Convicted 21 st March, 1842; confined three months —he is the person.)
GUILTY. Aged 13.— Judgment Respited.
[trialAccount t18460223-809]
809. WILLIAM FRANKLIN was indicted for stealing 32lbs. weight of lead, value 5 s., the goods of James Courthorpe Peache, and fixed to a building.—2nd COUNT, not stating it to be fixed.
THOMAS FILES . I am clerk to Mr. James Courthorpe Peache. I missed some lead from the corner of the counting-house on the 31st of Jan.—the officer brought the lead on the Monday following—we compared it, and it corresponded.
SAMUEL CORNELIUS . I am wharf foreman to Grissel and Peto. I was returning from dinner on the 31st of Jan., and the prisoner was pointed out to me as having a bulky appearance—I stopped him—I thought he had some tools of ours—I asked him what he had—he said, some lead he had found on the timber, and he produced one piece of lead—I said, I should call an officer—he then produced three other pieces—the counting-house where this lead was taken from, is five or six wharfs below ours, but those who gather the coals and other things walk through the mud.
WILLIAM GARRARD ( police-sergeant L 18.) I took the prisoner with this lead—I
saw it applied to the counting-house—it fitted exactly—he said he picked it up.
Prisoner. I was told by a boy that he knew where there was some lead, would I come and help him pick it up; we stopped near the Chalk-wharf; two other boys picked up some; this was all mud; I washed it off, and took it on shore; when I was locked up Brown was there; he asked me what I was in for; I said, "Some lead;" he said, "That is the lead that I and Maton stole," and he told me how he stole it. Witness. There was some wet mud on the lead—there is free access from that counting-house to the mud—he had 32lbs. of lead about him.
JURY. Q. Was there more lead missed than Brown had in his possessions? A. Yes, 100lbs. was missing—Brown was taken about half-past nine o'clock, about 400 yards from the wharf—it would have been easy for him to have thrown lead into the mud. ( See the last case.)
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18460223-810]
810. JOHN OLPIN was indicted for feloniously uttering a forged acceptance to a bill of exchange for 5 l. 5 s., with intent to defraud John Bartlett.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN BARTLETT . I am a broker, and live in Red-lion-court—I have known the prisoner about four years—on the 16th of July he came and requested of me to procure the sum of 5 l. for him, to enable him to pay his rent, and he gave me the name of Michael Coney—I let him have 2 l. on the 18th of July, for which he gave me an I O U, and the same day I gave him 3 l. 5 s., which made up 5 l. 5 s., and he gave me this bill of exchange for 5 l. 5 s., purporting to be accepted by Michael Coney, and the ink was not dry when he gave it me—I bud stated I would not advance the 5 l. 5 s. unless I had some security.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. You saw Coney before you lent the money? A. Yes—he said he had no objection to be bound for Olpin in the sum of 2 l. or 50 s.—he thought I came from a loan society, but I explained that I did not—here is the bill, and a eross under the words accepted M. Coney, and the ink was not dry when I had it for the money in Olpin's house—it purports to bear date 19th of July, and it was given me on the 18th—here is Olpin's indorsement on it—the acceptor cannot write—the ink-stand and pen were on the table in Olpin's house—it was about two o'clock in the afternoon—I stepped in quite accidentally, and he said, "It is all ready," or else I did not expect it till the following day.
COURT. Q. You went to the prisoner's house, he produced this cross, which was wet, and which purports to be the cross of Coney? Yes—I inquired if Coney was there, and the prisoner said he was just gone.
MR. PARRY. Q. You took the bill away with you? A. Yes—when it became due the prisoner either called or sent to me to request me to hold it, and I wrote to Coney—I saw Coney about a month after it became due, and two months after it was given to me, and he told me it was a forgery—Olpin then told me it was accepted by Coney's son—I went to his son, and he denied it—when the bill was due I did not present it to the acceptor—I held it over for a month before I saw him—the money I lent belonged to Mr. Palmer, whose business I was transacting, and I communicated the fact to him—I applied to him for permission to lend to Olpin—I did not consider I was the person to prosecute the prisoner—Mr. Palmer gave him into custody.
MICHAEL CONEY . I live in James-place, Gravel-lane, and am a hair and size manufacturer—Mr. Bartlett came to me in July, about being security for Olpin for five guineas—I said, "If it were my own father, I would not"—I did not mind 50 s., but no more—he sent me notice afterwards, and I went to Olpin, and said what did he bring my name in question for—he said there was some difference about Mr. Bartlett, and there was some bill—I went away and heard no more—Mr. Bartlett afterwards came and said, "About that bill of 5 l. 5 s."—I said, "I know nothing about it"—he went back to Olpin, and he told him it was my son—Mr. Bartlett came back and said, "It is your son"—I asked my son if he did it—he said he did not—it passed on from that time till the prisoner was taken to Union-hall, and they sent for me—I went, and said to the prisoner, "Did I do it for you"—"No," says he, "your son did it"—my son was not at home—I did not make this cross on this bill—I did not authorise any person to do it.
Cross-examined. Q. Can you read this bill? A. No—I do not know the contents of it—when I said I would not mind being answerable for 50 s. I was not told I should have to give a written paper—I am not in the habit of making marks—I was security for Olpin to a loan society with another man for 5 l. or 6 l.—I laid hold of the pen then, and the clerk wrote my name—I did not make a cross—I have known the prisoner seven or eight years—I have seen, him as I have any other person in the street—I do not become security to lost societies for any other person I see in the street—I have only been security for him once—I believe that is twelve months ago—I will swear that on the 18th of July I was not at Olpin's house—I cannot tell what day of the week the 18th of July was—I will swear before God and man, that on no day about the middle of July I was at Olpin's house—I can do that honestly—I never was in any trouble—I never was in a court-house in my life, I am quite certain—I have never been in this gaol—I have never been indicted in this court or any other—I never heard of an indictment for perjury against me—I mean to swear I have never been indicted for perjury—I never was under a lock in my life yet, except my own door—I never dealt in cart-grease—I never was indicted for swearing that I did not receive a load of cart-grease, whereas I did receive it—I do not know Mr. Margeson of the new leather-market, Bermondsey—I will swear he never indicted me for perjury, nor anybody else, thank God—after this bill became due I did not go to Olpin's and say, "You had better say my son accepted it to get a little more time"—I am certain of that, and I never saw the bill till I saw it with Mr. Palmer.
PATRICK CONEY . I am the son of Michael Coney—I never put this mark on this bill.
Cross-examined. Q. Can you read and write? A. I can read very well—I can write as much as my own name.
COURT. Q. Do you remember your father being charged with perjury? A. No, I never remember his being tried for it—I never heard of it.
MR. PARRY. Q. Just remember—do not you recollect his being indicted for perjury, and being committed to gaol for it? A. No, I do not—I do not know Mr. Margeson of the new leather-market, Bermondsey—I do not remember my father ever being in trouble, or in gaol since I have known him—I have never been in trouble myself.
ISAIAH COHEN ( police-constable M 210.) I took the prisoner—he said it was a bona fide transaction.
JAMES PALMER examined by MR. PARRY. I live in Victoria-place, Stoke Newington-road—I was brought up to the watch trade—I have been a private gentleman for twenty-seven years—I do not do anything for my living—I lend a little money out now and then—I do not make a good deal by lending out
money because I do not do it to a great extent—I do it now and then—I do not know Olpin except through this transaction—I know Bartlett—he is my friend, and he is my agent in matters of this kind—I advanced this money to Mr. Bartlett, and he obtained the bill and said that was good security, presuming the acceptance was all correct.
COURT. Q. What money did you advance? A. 5 l.—I was to have 5 l. 5 s. for it for one month—I do not know what that is per cent.—I am not scholar enough for that—we do things off-hand at once, and do not calculate the interest—I was told in Sept. last that this was a forgery.
MR. PARRY. Q. If this money had been paid he never would have been indicted? A. No, there would have been an end of the transaction—whether there would be an end of it if he would pay me now, I must leave to others.
MR. PARRY called
SOPHIA OLPIN . I am thirteen years old—I am the prisoner's daughter. I know Mr. Coney—he and my father have been acquainted long—in June or July I saw Coney at our house—it was just before we had our dinner, or directly after—I do not remember the day of the month when he came in—a bit of paper was lying on the table—my father was writing when Mr. Coney came in, and said, "Olpin, I can't write, but you write my name; I will put my cross, that will do just the same," and he did so—it was a strip of paper like this.
MR. WILDE. Q. Do you write yourself? A. Yes—I was standing in the room against the door—I do not know whether it was before dinner or directly after—no one dined with us that day—the first time I saw the paper, it was on the table—I saw Coney come into the room—neither the dinnerthingsnor the tea-things were on the table—the door was open, and my father and Mr. Coney walked in together—they took chairs, and sat down by the side of one another, and then the paper was lying on the table—my father laid it on the table—I cannot exactly say where he brought it from—it was lying on the top of the books—I did not see what was on it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18460223-811]
811. GEORGE REASON was indicted , for that he on the 6th of Jan., and a certain man, whose name is unknown, in and upon Mary Collins, feloniously did make an assault, putting her in corporal fear and danger of her life, and feloniously did rob and steal from her person, and against her will, 1 frying-pan, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 s.; her property; and immediately before, at the time of, and immediately after the said robbery, did beat, strike, and use personal violence to her: and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MARY COLLINS . I am single, and live in Tyer's-street, Vauxhall. On the 6th of Jan. I had been at work at Camberwell New-road—when I came out about seven or eight o'clock at night, I saw the prisoner and another, whom I do not know, in the street—I had a basket on my head, and a frying-pan—the prisoner put his arms round me, and pushed me against the gate in the road—I went in and waited a little while till I thought they were gone—I then went on, and saw them again—they used very bad language, and as I got to Kennington-oval, they came up to me—the other one laid he would rip my b—y guts open—he put an open knife against my breast—he took it out, opened it, and put it against my breast—he said something about money, but I was frightened and do not know what he said—he then dropped the knife, and kicked me violently in the lower part of the belly—it gave me so much pain, I could not stand upright—he then held me, while the prisoner gave me two or three blows in the side, and then kicked me—he
snatched the basket from me, and looked into it—he threw it back to me—there was not anything worth while in it—he then snatched the frying-pan from me, and rolled me in the mud—he put his hand into my pocket—I said, "Don't rob me"—he struck me again between the shoulders—he said, "You old b—, I have done for you"—they then ran away round the Oval—I felt in my pocket directly, and missed a shilling, which I know was safe after they first spoke to me—I was going round the Oval, and saw the other man—he whistled, and then called, " Punch, it is all right"—the prisoner then came up and joined the other man—the prisoner saw me, and said, "You old b—, I thought I had done for you"—then they went away together.
Prisoner. I know nothing about it. Witness. I am certain he is the man if I were to die this minute—I am well known in Lambeth—I had never seen him before.
Prisoner. On the 31st of Jan. I came down Kennington-lane; this woman came and said, "That is Punch, I think it is him; "she said I took a fryingpan out of her hand, and lifted up her gown, took a shilling out of her pocket, and then she said I took a basket out of her hand; there was nothing in it, and I threw it at her, and she said the other one did nothing to her; I was remanded four times, and she never came, and said nothing till last Monday; I do not know her at all.
THOMAS FARMER WHITCOMB ( police-constable L 89.) I took the prisoner—I showed him and three others to Collins, and she identified him as being the person who robbed her—she said he had sore eyes, and she picked him out—he goes by the name of Punch.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing about it; I think the policeman has put it into her head, to get a few shillings; because I go by the name of Punch, if there is anything done, I am to be taken for it.
SAMUEL HOCKADAY ( police-constable L 10.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction by the name of James Purdy—( read—Convicted 3 rd Feb., 1845, and confined six months)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, 30 TH OF MARCH.
